A third consumer then appeared that is not a call site at all - `Core.StallBudgetSeconds` needs the *resolved* download timeout without issuing a request - and two call sites already shared an identical ceiling, so the numbers moved into the helper as `ApiCeilingSeconds`/`ApiUnconfiguredSeconds`/`FileTransferCeilingSeconds` (`WebRequestExtensions.cs:21`, `WebRequestExtensions.cs:45`) behind two named policies, `EffectiveApiTimeout` and `EffectiveFileTransferTimeout` (`WebRequestExtensions.cs:84`, `WebRequestExtensions.cs:93`). The five call sites now name a policy instead of passing numbers; the pure `EffectiveTimeout` (`WebRequestExtensions.cs:74`) is still the primitive underneath and everything below about *which* numbers and *why* is unchanged. The helper is `internal`, not `public`: nothing outside the framework assembly touches a `UnityWebRequest`, and an `internal` helper can change signature for years where a public one in the global namespace cannot.<br><br>**Neither the ceilings nor the defaults are equal across the blocks, and getting the *default* wrong was the mistake review caught.** The first version parameterised only the ceiling and hardcoded a shared 30s fallback, so `timeout: 0` on a download block resolved to **30** - shorter than the shipped 60, and the shortest bound in the framework - on precisely the requests that cannot resume. `code-reviewer` and `architect-reviewer` reached that independently and both named the same fix: on those blocks 0 has always meant "no limit", so the nearest bounded reading of it is the **ceiling**, not the smallest timeout available. `Api` keeps its own 30, because there 0 means "unset". Both numbers are arguments now, and the probe reads the call sites' *source* to check each download site passes its ceiling in both positions - a call-site fact no runtime assertion can see.<br><br>**On the ceilings themselves:** `Api` keeps 300 (`WebRequestExtensions.cs:21`) and the two download blocks share **3600** (`WebRequestExtensions.cs:45`) - one constant rather than the two identical copies they had until v0.49.10. Applying Api's 300 to `AssetDownload` would have been a regression rather than a unification: `www.timeout` bounds the *whole* request, and a failed attempt calls `TryDeletePartialFile`, so **nothing resumes** - every retry restarts at byte zero. A ceiling below the honest transfer time therefore does not slow a large asset, it makes it permanently undownloadable, and the only trace is one "Giving up" line. The ceiling's job here is to turn an unbounded wait into a finite one, not to impose a policy; Api can afford a policy because a JSON server that has gone quiet for five minutes is not going to answer.<br><br>**A second, unlogged defect fell out of the measurement.** The old lines cast a `uint` settings field with `(int)`. `(int)uint.MaxValue` is `-1`, and UnityWebRequest stores a negative timeout as **0** - measured, not reasoned - so an over-large value landed on precisely the unbounded wait it looked like it was avoiding. The helper takes a `long` and clamps *before* narrowing, so both an `int` and a `uint` field convert implicitly and neither can wrap.<br><br>**Verified 2026-08-17, 17 of 17 assertions plus 3 source checks**, and unusually for this codebase the whole table is reachable in the editor - the resolver is a pure static, so no `Core.settings`, no play mode and no arranged failure. Five are counter-tests: the shipped 60 reaches `www.timeout` as 60 on both download blocks, Api's shipped 30 as 30, a value sitting exactly on the ceiling is not capped, and a legitimate 1200s asset wait survives (it would be 300 under Api's policy). One asserts the *rejected* design explicitly - a download 0 must not resolve to 30 - because a clamp that is merely present looks identical to one that is right. The probe also reads each call site's ceiling off its own private const by reflection, so it cannot pass while a site names the wrong one | `WebRequestExtensions.cs:74`, `WebRequestExtensions.cs:84`, `WebRequestExtensions.cs:93`, `WebRequestExtensions.cs:100`, `WebRequestExtensions.cs:106`, `Api.cs:250`, `AssetManager.cs:1017`, `AssetManager.cs:1223`, `TextureCacheManager.cs:479`, `TextureCacheManager.cs:770` | **fixed 2026-08-17** (v0.49.9) |

H17 family: the argument really is evaluated on Standalone and Android, where `DO_LOGS` is defined. | M88 | **Fixed, together with M89 - they were one cause.** The invalid-filetype exit in `Load(string)` raised the **wrong** event. With the shipped `showImageDownloadError: true` it displayed the placeholder through `SetImageTexture` / `SetRawImageTexture` - the success setters - so an unusable filetype announced `onLoadCompleteEvent`, a false success, and returned without ever invoking `onLoadFailedEvent`. "Raised neither event" was only true with that flag off; its two immediate neighbours both invoke and then `RemoveAllListeners`. `MediaVOExtensions.Load` registers both handlers before calling, so a caller waiting on `onLoadFailed` never heard - **and the `onLoadFailed` listener it registered was never invoked and never cleared**, surviving into the next `Load` on the same instance - so a gallery reusing one `DownloadableImage` fired item A's *failure* callback for item B. It is also why `Load(url, fallbackUrl)` never fell back on an unusable filetype: only the empty-url path reached the fallback.<br><br>There is now one terminal-failure exit, `FailLoad(reason)` - it clears **both** event lists, invokes `onLoadFailedEvent` and clears `_isLoading` - and every failing path routes through it. Clearing both is the half that matters: a load that has failed will never complete, so a surviving `onLoadCompleteEvent` listener is not waiting harmlessly, it is waiting for the *next* load. The re-entrancy guard is deliberately not routed through it, because there the failing call is the new one while a previous load is still in flight, and clearing `_isLoading` would cancel that load's bookkeeping<br><br>**Both entries described the mechanism wrongly when they were closed, and the wrong version reached a commit message before review caught it.** The claim was that the `Image` path diverged from the `RawImage` path - that `ImageLoaderThread` deferred the callback, inverting the event order and dropping the completion event. It does not: `ImageLoaderThread.LoadImage` is synchronous and says so in its own remarks, and `MainController` enqueues straight into it. `architect-reviewer` and `silent-failure-hunter` found that independently. There was one bug on two identical paths, not two symptoms of one cause. Corrected in v0.44.0. | `DownloadableImage.cs:319`, `DownloadableImage.cs:542` | **fixed 2026-08-14** (v0.43.0) |
| M89 | **Fixed. The failure path was travelling through the success callback.** With `showImageDownloadError` on - which ships - a failed download displayed the placeholder by calling the loader `callback`, and that callback *is* the success path: `SetRawImageTexture` and `SetImageSprite` both raise and clear `onLoadCompleteEvent`. So a failure announced completion. It did so synchronously, before `onLoadFailedEvent`, on **both** graphic paths - so a caller using `onLoadComplete` to hide a spinner or count a loaded item recorded the failure as a success. If `SerialPort` was nulled concurrently, the `SerialPort.IsOpen` test inside that same catch NREs and kills the read thread silently.<br><br>Deliberately left open rather than patched blind: clearing `isRunning` on *any* exception would stop the loop on the first read **timeout**, and a timeout is normal traffic on a serial port. Distinguishing "the device went away" from "nothing arrived this cycle" is the actual work - probably a consecutive-failure count like `AssetManager`'s `ConsecutiveFailureLimit`, which was measured rather than guessed (M34). Also in scope when someone takes it: `OnDestroy` unsubscribes events but neither closes the port nor stops the thread, so a destroyed component leaves the thread reading and COM held<br><br>**Fixed 2026-08-17 (v0.49.13), and the decision this entry was waiting for is a consecutive *non-timeout* failure count.** `TimeoutException` is caught separately and deliberately **not** counted: a read timeout means nothing arrived this cycle, which is the resting state of most serial devices, so counting it would stop a healthy port - exactly the blind patch the entry refused. Anything else, with the port genuinely open, increments; a completed read resets the run, and so does each loop start. Under the limit the failure is reported and reading continues, because a USB adapter re-enumerating can throw a few times and recover. At the limit `isRunning` is cleared and the loop reports, through one exit, `FailReadLoop`. Be honest about what clearing it buys: **nothing re-reads `IsRunning` after boot**, so the two `LogError` lines are the actual report and the flag only matters to a project that polls it.<br><br>**The limit is 5 failures AND a 2s window, and the first version had only the count - which review showed made its own comment false.** Five iterations is microseconds on a thread and about 83 ms over five frames as a coroutine, so "chosen to survive a USB re-enumeration" was an order of magnitude out; a re-enumeration takes hundreds of milliseconds. Both conditions must now hold. The numbers are still **reasoned, not measured** - `AssetManager`'s `ConsecutiveFailureLimit` of 3 came from a real run (M34) and there is no serial adapter here to unplug.<br><br>**The NRE half is fixed and measured.** The catch now captures `SerialPort` into a local before testing it: `StopSerialThreading` sets that field to null while this thread may be inside the catch, so the old `SerialPort.IsOpen` was a second dereference of a field that had just become null - it threw out of the catch and killed the read thread with nothing logged. A null or closed port now returns quietly and is **not** counted, because that is what a deliberate stop looks like from inside the loop.<br><br>**Four more things review found, and two of them made the first version of this fix useless.**<br><br>**(a) The quiet return did not clear `isRunning`, and inferred intent from `IsOpen`.** A removed adapter's stream disposes itself, so `IsOpen` goes false *with no exception* - indistinguishable from a deliberate close if `IsOpen` is all you look at. The loop returned quietly and then span forever on the open check with no count and no log, `IsRunning` still true: **M101 closed on paper and unchanged in fact, for exactly the failure it was written for.** Intent now comes from an explicit `stopRequested` flag set by the two stop methods. My own probe asserted the buggy behaviour as correct, which is the third time this session a counter-test agreed with the defect.<br><br>**(b) `ParseSerialData` ran inside the read's `try`**, and it raises `SerialDataParseEvent` - so an App subscriber throwing was reported as `Serial read failed`, pointing an operator at the cable for someone else's bug, at Error, once per message, forever. It could not even reach the limit, because the success reset ran before the parse and the count stuck at 1. Hoisted out, with its own guard naming it a consumer fault.<br><br>**(c) `CloseSerialPort` stopped the loop *after* its catch**, so a `Close()` that threw - which is what a removed adapter does - escaped with the loop still running and the handle still held, defeating the `OnDestroy` change in the one case it was added for. The loop is stopped first now, and that catch's `notificationManager` is null-guarded like its sibling.<br><br>**(d) The notification marshalling is dormant, not a live fix.** `LoopMethod` is never assigned anywhere, so the `Coroutine` initializer always wins and the read thread is unreachable today. Kept as a guard against a future assignment, and the comment says so rather than claiming a thread-safety bug was fixed. `OnDestroy` closing the port is the one here that was plainly broken and is plainly better.<br><br>**Verified 2026-08-17: 8 runtime assertions and 7 source checks**, after the expectations were inverted. Measured: a vanished port now **clears** `isRunning` and reports it; a *requested* stop stays silent and is not treated as a fault; a closed-but-present port with no stop requested is also treated as gone; `StopSerialCoroutine` sets the flag; and giving up needs both a count and a duration. Three of the eight are counter-tests. **Unverified, and it is most of the policy**: the count reaching the limit against real hardware, the window's value, and the teardown - all need an adapter to unplug. **Dormant here regardless**: `SerialPorts.isActive` ships `false`. | `UnitySerialPort.cs:172`, `UnitySerialPort.cs:182`, `UnitySerialPort.cs:207`, `UnitySerialPort.cs:1076`, `UnitySerialPort.cs:988`, `UnitySerialPort.cs:1052`, `UnitySerialPort.cs:723`, `UnitySerialPort.cs:419` | **fixed 2026-08-17** (v0.49.13), dormant behind `SerialPorts.isActive: false` |
| M102 | **The `assetManager` half of M78, and it cannot take M78's fix.** `CoreSystemUI.Awake` resolves `assetManager` with `FindAnyObjectByType`, but `MainController` **creates** one when the scene has none - and unlike the texture manager, which is created inside `MainController.Init` and so exists by the time `systemUI.Init()` runs, `SetupAssetManager` is called from `AppDataController_OnDataLoadedEvent` (`MainController.cs:615`), which is asynchronous and **after** `Init` on the remote path - though note the local-appData path raises it synchronously *before* `Init`, which is what made the first fix a regression. So re-resolving in `Init` - what fixed M78 - still finds null here, and the download and verification readout is absent for the entire first-run download. v0.49.6 made the attempt report rather than fail silently; v0.49.7 makes it work, and that report is gone with the branch it lived on.<br><br>**Latent here**, because `CoreSetup.unity` binds `assetManager`; live for any branched project whose scene does not.<br><br>**The fix has a hook and needs no new API.** `Core.main.AppDataController` is public and `OnDataLoadedEvent` is a public event, and `SetupAppData` runs at `MainController.cs:348` - before `Core.cs:312` calls `systemUI.Init()` - so the controller already exists when `CoreSystemUI.Init` runs and can be subscribed there. `MainController` subscribes first, so its handler has created the `AssetManager` before any later subscriber runs, and `SetupAssetManager_IE` waits 2.5s before validating, which is ample slack to catch `onTotalAssetsEvent`. Found by `silent-failure-hunter`, which also noted the tidier alternative: give `MainController` a public `AssetManager` property mirroring `TextureCacheManager`, since the field is `private` today.<br><br>**One caveat, and it needed a guard after all**: with `AppData.isActive` false, `AppDataController` is null *and* `SetupAssetManager` never runs, so the hook's availability coincides with the phase existing - but `WaitForAssetManager` is reached under `AssetDownload.isActive` alone, so without an `AppData.isActive` term it reported a missing readout for a phase that could not run. The term is in place and counter-tested<br><br>**Fixed 2026-08-17 (v0.49.7).** `Init` no longer gives up: when `assetManager` is null it subscribes to `AppDataController.OnDataLoadedEvent` and wires the readout when the manager appears (`CoreSystemUI.cs:182`, `CoreSystemUI.cs:205`). `MainController` gained a read-only `AssetManager` accessor (`MainController.cs:76`) - a property over the existing `[SerializeField] private` field rather than an auto-property, because Unity does not serialize auto-properties. **Additive public API**, so nothing breaks, but the XML doc warns that it is null for most of the boot.<br><br>**The first version of this fix was a REGRESSION, caught by review and blocked before commit.** It only ever waited for the event, and on the local-appData path the event has already fired: with `useLocalAppData` on Standalone, `AppDataController.Init` reads the file and raises `OnDataLoadedEvent` **synchronously**, inside `MainController.SetupAppData` and therefore inside `main.Init()` - which `Core` runs *before* `systemUI.Init()`. So `MainController` had already created the manager and unsubscribed, and `CoreSystemUI` then subscribed to an event that would never fire again: the readout stranded, with **no log at all** - strictly worse than the defect it replaced, which at least reported. `Init` now resolves from `Core.main.AssetManager` first (`CoreSystemUI.cs:120`) and only waits if still null. Resolve first, wait second.<br><br>**Subscription order is what makes the waiting half work, and it is asserted rather than assumed.** `MainController` subscribes during its own `Init` (`MainController.cs:576`), which `Core` runs before `systemUI.Init()`, so the handler that creates the manager is always registered first and a C# event invokes in subscription order. The probe registers a listener after `CoreSystemUI`'s and confirms the manager is already non-null by then.<br><br>**Two things it deliberately stays quiet about.** A repeat appData load cannot re-wire the listeners, because the handler unsubscribes on first fire; and it does not report when `Core.SkipAssetValidation` is set, because `MainController`'s handler then goes straight to `LoadNextScene` and never creates a manager - there is no readout to miss and nothing is wrong. Any other route to a missing manager still reports at `LogError`.<br><br>`OnDisable`, not `OnDestroy` (`CoreSystemUI.cs:258`): it covers deactivation too, and a delegate left on the controller's event by an inactive component still fires into it. Guarded by a flag so it only unsubscribes when there is something to unsubscribe.<br><br>Verified 13/13, including three counter-tests: it reports when the manager is still absent after the load, stays silent when validation was skipped, and `OnDisable` drops the listener count | `CoreSystemUI.cs:182`, `CoreSystemUI.cs:205`, `CoreSystemUI.cs:258`, `MainController.cs:76`, `MainController.cs:615` | **fixed 2026-08-17** (v0.49.7) |

| M106 | **A clamped timeout is applied silently, so a "Request timeout" log cannot be reconciled with the `settings.json` that caused it.** `WebRequestExtensions` rewrites an operator's value - 0 up to the ceiling, 86400 down to it - and nothing anywhere reports that it did. The *failure* is loud: `AssetManager` logs `Download failed [...], attempt 1 of 4` per attempt and `Giving up on [...] It will be missing at runtime.`, all `Development.LogError` and all always compiled. What is missing is **attribution** - no line states the timeout actually in force, so a timeout against a file reading `86400` is not reconcilable by the person holding both.<br><br>**The log cannot go in `SetTimeout`**, which is why this is an entry and not a one-liner: there it would fire once per asset, 200 lines for 200 assets. And every cheap channel is stripped - `Development.LogWarning` and `LogIf` are both `[Conditional("DO_LOGS")]`, and `ProjectSettings.asset` defines `DO_LOGS` for `Android` and `Standalone` - where **Standalone is Windows, macOS and Linux**, not Windows alone - so they are stripped on **iOS and WebGL only**. That is a smaller hole than the "four targets" this file claimed elsewhere for a year, and it does not rescue a `LogIf`: `AssetDownload.doLogs` and `TextureCache.doLogs` both ship `false`, so on every target that does compile it, the call is gated off anyway. It has to be one ungated report at settings-parse or boot time, naming key, typed value and applied value. `SettingsParse.Error` is the right shape and already reports at `Debug.LogError`, but it is wired only to the settings panel's `SetData`, and `timeout` appears in no `SetData` at all - so today there is no path by which an operator can be told their timeout did not take. **Live**, on the shipped blocks, though the shipped 60/60/30 are never clamped so nothing would fire until someone edits them. At `Development.LogError`, which is the only level always compiled - and the message says *why*, distinguishing the three cases that reach it: a 0 that resolves to the ceiling, a value above the ceiling, and - reachable only on `Api.timeout`, which is a signed `int` - a negative number, which would otherwise have been explained as though it were 0 (`Core.cs:468`).<br><br>**Where it is gated, and one place it deliberately is not.** `Api` is gated on `Api.isActive` and the download blocks on `AppData.isActive`, because an operator who switched a module off does not need an error about a number it will never read. `AssetDownload` is additionally gated on its own `isActive`. **`TextureCache` is not**: that flag gates the boot pre-cache pass, not `TextureCacheLoader`, which downloads and serves with the flag off - so a clamp on that block is live either way and gating it here would hide the half that runs.<br><br>**Verified 2026-08-17, 15 of 15** (shared with M107). The report is observed through `Application.logMessageReceived` rather than by reading the code, and four of the assertions are counter-tests: the shipped 60/60/30 produces **silence**, an inactive `Api` produces silence, `AppData` off silences both download blocks, and `TextureCache` off still reports. Three bad values produce exactly three lines, one per key | `Core.cs:428`, `Core.cs:468`, `Core.cs:306` | **fixed 2026-08-17** (v0.49.10) |
| M107 | **The boot-stall watchdog sizes its budget from a timeout that no longer bounds anything, so a mistyped value disarms the loudest diagnostic in the project.** `Core.BootWatchdog_IE` reads `AppData.Cache.AssetDownload.timeout` raw and sets `stallBudget = Max(90, timeout * 2)`, on the sound reasoning that the budget must clear the longest silent gap a *healthy* boot can leave. Since M43 the request is capped at 3600 while the budget still scales off the unclamped number - so `timeout: 86400` caps requests at an hour and gives `BOOT STALLED` a **48-hour** deadline. A hung boot then looks like a slow one, and the symptom is the *absence* of a log line, which nobody notices.<br><br>**Not caused by M43 and not fixed by it** - before the clamp the request was unbounded *and* the budget was enormous, so the watchdog was equally useless; M43 only makes the fix cheap. **Deliberately left out of v0.49.9**: it changes boot-watchdog timing, which deserves its own test rather than riding along in a timeout commit, and there is a trade-off to settle first. Reading the effective value is correct by the comment's own logic, but it also means a project configuring 0 gets a 2-hour budget - accurate, and indistinguishable from a watchdog that is off. The likely answer is to read the effective value *and* have M106's report tell the operator to write a real number. Needs `AssetManager.MaxTimeoutSeconds` to stop being `private`, or `EffectiveTimeout` to be called from `Core`. Found by `code-reviewer` and `silent-failure-hunter` independently<br><br>**Fixed 2026-08-17 (v0.49.10), and the entry above was wrong about what actually happened.** It said a mistyped 86400 gave `BOOT STALLED` a 48-hour deadline. It did not, because the configured value was **never read at all**. The budget was computed once, before the loop, and this coroutine is started at `Core.cs` *before* the settings parse - deliberately, since that parse failing is the most common way this framework hangs and is what the watchdog exists to catch. Everything before a coroutine's first `yield` runs on the first `MoveNext` (measured), which Unity performs synchronously inside `StartCoroutine` (documented; the play-mode step is in the test plan). So `Core.settings` was null every time, `requestTimeout` was 0, and the budget was **always the 90s floor** - the headroom its own comment promised had never once been applied.<br><br>**Which means the live risk was the opposite of the one filed.** Not a watchdog that cannot fire, but one that fires *falsely*: any project with a request timeout above 45s - and the shipped value is 60 - could exceed a 90s stall budget on one slow asset, and the watchdog **latches**, so a false fire refuses to boot at all. The fix is correct under either reading, which is why it is one change: `StallBudgetSeconds` (`Core.cs:343`) is called on **every** deadline reset (`Core.cs:507`), returns the floor while settings are null, and scales off the *effective* timeout so a capped value cannot inflate it.<br><br>**The 0 case is a deliberate trade, not an oversight.** `timeout: 0` means "no limit" on that block and resolves to the 3600s ceiling, so the budget becomes 7200 - which looks indistinguishable from a disabled watchdog. It is nonetheless the honest number: a request really can take that long, and reporting sooner would report a healthy boot as a failure. M106 is what makes it visible, which is why the two were done together rather than M107 alone.<br><br>**One budget still covers every stage**, including the ones that issue no request. That is coarse - a settings parse does not need an hour - and narrowing it would mean turning the stage strings into an enum. Left as is: a late report is a far better failure than a false one.<br><br>**Verified 2026-08-17, 15 of 15** (shared with M106), including the assertion that would have failed before this change - the shipped `timeout: 60` must now yield a 120s budget rather than 90 | `Core.cs:343`, `Core.cs:399`, `Core.cs:507` | **fixed 2026-08-17** (v0.49.10) |
| M108 | **`TextureCacheManager`'s own stall watchdog used a flat 60s while the timeout it has to clear can legally be an hour.** Same defect as M107, in the sibling: `CacheStallTimeoutSeconds` was `60f` and used verbatim, but no progress mark can arrive faster than one request, and `TextureCache.timeout` may be up to the 3600s ceiling. With `timeout: 120` a single slow image exceeds the budget, the pass `break`s, and `RaiseVerificationComplete()` runs with a half-empty cache. It now returns the floor when the block is null. Found by `silent-failure-hunter` while reviewing M107 | `TextureCacheManager.cs:37`, `TextureCacheManager.cs:31`, `TextureCacheManager.cs:341` | **fixed 2026-08-17** (v0.49.10) |
| M109 | **The texture pre-cache is a single boot stage covering every image, so no budget derived from one request is honest for it.** `MarkBootStage` is called twice around the pass - `"caching textures"` (`MainController.cs:704`) and `"textures cached"` (`MainController.cs:712`) - and `TextureCacheManager` never calls it at all. So `Core`'s stall watchdog sees one stage string for the whole pass: a healthy 300-image pre-cache holds it for minutes, against a budget of twice one request timeout. The watchdog latches, so a false fire makes `LoadNextScene` refuse permanently.<br><br>**Not caused by M107, and M107's fix does not resolve it** - it improves the magnitude (the budget was effectively a flat 90s before) while leaving the shape wrong. The comment on the `TextureCache` branch of `StallBudgetSeconds` (`Core.cs:342`) now says so explicitly rather than claiming the pass marks its own stages, which an earlier draft of it did and which review disproved by grepping every call site.<br><br>**The real fix is to mark per-image progress**, exactly as the asset pass does through `MarkBootStage($"assets {done}/{total}", log: false)` - the `log: false` overload exists for this - driven off `onTextureCachedEvent`, which `MainController` already subscribes to. **Dormant as shipped**: `TextureCache.isActive` is `false`, so the pre-cache does not run. Live for any project that turns it on with more than a handful of images. Found by `code-reviewer` reviewing M107<br><br>**Fixed 2026-08-18 (v0.52.4) exactly as this entry prescribed**: `MainController` now subscribes to `TextureCacheManager.onTextureCachedEvent` - which the manager already raised as `(imageDictionary.Count, url, mediaAssets.Count)` and nobody had ever subscribed to - and marks `$"textures {done}/{total}"` per image. `"caching textures"` survives as a starting mark, immediately superseded. Mirrors `AssetManager_onAssetProgress` including the part that matters: the mark is **unconditional** and only its log line follows `doLogs`, because M107's round established that gating the whole call puts the shipped default straight back into a false stall.<br><br>**One caveat, written into the handler rather than fixed.** `done` is `imageDictionary.Count`, which only moves on a **distinct** url - so a run of duplicates leaves the stage string unchanged and the deadline unreset. Acceptable, because a duplicate is a dictionary hit rather than a download and so cannot be the slow case; recorded because "the counter did not move" and "nothing is happening" are indistinguishable from the watchdog's side, which is the whole subject of this entry.<br><br>**Verified: 10 assertions**, and the one that carries it is not "the handler exists". `MarkBootStage` resets the deadline only when the stage **string** changes (M70), so the probe drives `Core.MarkBootStage` with the strings this handler emits and asserts the recorded stage really changes between consecutive images - plus the **counter-test** that marking the *same* string twice leaves it unchanged, which is the duplicate-url case above, demonstrated rather than assumed. `_bootStage` is restored and the restore is asserted.<br><br>**Still dormant**: `TextureCache.isActive` ships `false`, so the pre-cache does not run. Live for any project that turns it on with more than a handful of images - which is precisely who this entry was written for<br><br>**Review found the first version fixed the wrong half, and that is the important part of this entry.** `onTextureCachedEvent` was raised **only on `Result.Success`**; the failure branch and the skips advanced the counter silently. The off-by-one went with it: the count is post-increment, so the sequence is 1..N and the last item marks `N/N`, where reading `imageDictionary.Count` before the `Add` ran 0..N-1, never reached the total, and understated the figure printed in `BOOT STALLED`.<br><br>**`Core.StallBudgetSeconds`' rationale updated with it.** It still said the pass "marks exactly two stages", which was the premise for calling twice-one-timeout a lower bound. That premise is now false, and the comment says both what makes the budget honest and that the load-bearing word is *processed*, not *cached* - so moving the mark back onto the success path silently invalidates it.<br><br>**My own comment documented the wrong risk**: it named duplicate urls as the stall case, but a duplicate is a dictionary hit and cannot be slow, and `mediaAssets` is de-duplicated upstream. That pair proves the *shadowed* copy went rather than the shadowing one; a census alone cannot separate those, and getting it backwards would have silently changed what the menu creates. 10 assertions each side.<br><br>**Counter-tests**: `Descriptor.asset` still loads as `EnigmaVirtual.ApplicationDescription`; `Resources.Load` of both prefabs sharing the `Gema/` folder still resolves; four assemblies `0 Error(s)` present; zero console entries newer than the build.<br><br>**The cross-assembly duplicate shape is now closed at four instances** (L22, L23, L27, M118) - verified by two independent instruments, a source parse over 287 files and an enumeration of the loaded domain, the latter seeing precompiled DLLs a parser cannot. All of it is rasterised at runtime from `Assets/05_Fonts/HelveticaBold.ttf` (307,936 B, present today). Delete or fail to pack that one file and the operator-facing error notification renders as spaces - `m_missingGlyphCharacter: 0` substitutes space, not a box - which means **the mechanism that reports problems becomes the thing that fails silently**.<br><br>**Live but not currently broken**: the `.ttf` is packed today as a dependency of `CoreSetup.unity`. This is a fragility entry, not a present malfunction, and the fix is a guard rather than a change. **The cheap guard is one `BuildValidator` rule**: every `m_AtlasPopulationMode: 1` font asset must have a resolvable `m_SourceFontFile` whose importer sets `includeFontData: 1`. Three lines of `AssetDatabase.FindAssets("t:TMP_FontAsset")`, and it fails loudly at build time instead of silently at a site. Nothing in `BuildValidator` catches this today.<br><br>Found by `silent-failure-hunter` 2026-08-19 while confirming L32's fix reached a player<br><br>**Fixed 2026-08-19 (v0.55.2): `BuildValidator` now refuses a build when a Dynamic TMP font asset cannot reach its source.** `CheckDynamicFontSources` scans `AssetDatabase.FindAssets("t:TMP_FontAsset")` and reports two conditions - no source of any kind, or a source whose importer has *Include Font Data* disabled, since `FontEngine` rasterises from the embedded bytes. Registered as an **always** rule rather than release-only: a development build with blank text is exactly as broken, and this is the class of defect found late precisely because it looks like a layout problem.<br><br>**The judgement is split into `DescribeDynamicFontProblem(font, path)` so it can be driven directly**, which is what made the counter-tests possible without running a build. Two legitimate shapes must NOT be refused, and both are asserted: a **Static** asset nulls its runtime source on purpose so the font is not dragged into the build, so it is not checked at all; and a Dynamic asset may be backed by an **OS font** through `m_SourceFontFilePath`. A gate that refuses too much is indistinguishable from a working one from the pass side, which is why those two carry as much weight here as the failing case.<br><br>**Tested: 18 assertions, 0 fail.** All three real font assets produce no complaint, including the Static one. A synthetic Dynamic asset with no source is reported, and the message names the path and `M120`. A synthetic Static asset with no source is **not** reported; nor is a Dynamic one with an OS-font path; nor does a null asset throw. The `includeFontData` branch was exercised against a **disposable copy** of a `.ttf` in a temp folder - `includeFontData = true` stays quiet, `false` is reported - with the folder deleted and the real font's importer verified untouched afterwards. Probing a tracked asset was avoided deliberately: the L32 round dirtied a tracked font asset twice.<br><br>**One API guess was wrong and compiling caught it.** There is no public `sourceFontFilePath` on `TMP_FontAsset` in this version - the OS-font path is the private serialized field `m_SourceFontFilePath`, reached by reflection in `ReadOsFontPath`. "The API exists" and "the API exists in this version" are different questions and only the compiler settles the second.<br><br>**The validator is guarded against itself.** Both failure messages read `font.characterTable`, which can be null on a constructed asset, so both use `?.Count ?? 0` - a validator that throws during a build is a worse failure than the one it exists to report.<br><br>**What this does and does not close.** It closes the *reference* hazard for every dynamic font asset, which is what L32 was. It does not address `HelveticaBold SDF`'s narrow 65-glyph coverage or its empty fallback table - those are fine while the source is present, because a Dynamic asset rasterises on demand, and that is exactly what the gate now guarantees<br><br>**Review findings applied 2026-08-19 (v0.55.3), one of which mattered.** `FindAssets` was project-wide and **includes `Packages/`** - measured - so a package-owned Dynamic font asset with an unreachable source would have blocked every build with nothing an operator could fix, `PackageCache` being regenerated on resolve. Now scoped to `new[] { "Assets" }`. That was the difference between a gate and a gate somebody deletes, which is the property this file's own comments call disqualifying.<br><br>Four smaller ones, all applied: the glyph count now reports **0** when `m_ClearDynamicDataOnBuild` is set, because such an asset ships none however many the editor shows - and that is exactly L32's shape, where quoting the editor count understated the damage. The message offers **deleting** the asset as a third fix, and a `<remarks>` block states the ceiling: the rule judges every font asset under `Assets/`, not the build's dependency closure, so it can report an unreferenced leftover. Both fail-open paths - a null importer, and an absent `m_SourceFontFilePath` - now emit a `Debug.Log` naming what could not be judged, since a check that silently does not run is worth little the day it matters. And `DescribeDynamicFontProblem` is back to `private`: `internal` bought nothing, because an `eval` snippet cannot bind it either and the tests reach it by reflection, which works the same on a private member.<br><br>**A correction to my own comment**: `ReadOsFontPath` claimed returning empty "fails safe". It does so only in the false-negative direction - if TMP ever renames that field, the rule would report a path-backed Dynamic asset as sourceless, a **false positive that blocks a build**. The comment now says which direction it fails in, and the method logs when the field is missing.<br><br>**And a process failure of mine worth recording**: `clearDynamicDataOnBuild` is `internal` to `Unity.TextMeshPro`, whose `InternalsVisibleTo` list does not include `Assembly-CSharp-Editor`, so the first version of that change **did not compile** - and I reported it as building because my grep for `error CS` matched nothing under `-v:q`, which suppresses the detail line while the summary says `1 Error(s)`. CLAUDE.md already says to assert `0 Error(s)` is *present* rather than treating "no error lines matched" as a pass. Read by reflection now, as `ReadOsFontPath` does | `Assets/05_Fonts/HelveticaBold SDF.asset:107`, `:1336`, `Assets/05_Fonts/HelveticaBold.ttf`, `Assets/Editor/BuildValidator.cs:66`, `:436` (`CheckDynamicFontSources`), `:452` (`DescribeDynamicFontProblem`) | **fixed 2026-08-19** (v0.55.2) |
| M121 | **The console's non-collapsed draw path loops `for (var i = 0; i < log.count; i++)` and `log.count` is unbounded, so a message another system repeats every frame turns the console into a hang.** `ProcessLogItem` coalesces a log identical to the previous one by **incrementing `count` on the existing entry** instead of appending, and `TrimExcessLogs` is only called on the *else* branch - so `maxLogCount: 1000` caps the number of **entries** and never caps `count`. `DrawLog` then emits one `GUILayout.Label` per repetition, on every OnGUI pass, and OnGUI runs at least twice a frame. One message repeating at 60fps for an hour reaches **216,000 labels per pass**.<br><br>**Live in the shipped config, and the bad path is the default.** `settings.json` has `DebugSettings.Console.isActive: true`, and `isCollapsed` defaults to **false**, which is the branch that loops. What holds it back is `isVisible`, which defaults false - so nothing is paid until somebody opens the console, which is exactly what you do when a message is repeating. Degrades without bound over hours, which is the unattended-kiosk shape this register is full of.<br><br>**Fix is a clamp on the draw loop** - `Mathf.Min(log.count, n)` - or dropping the repeat loop entirely and always showing the count badge the collapsed path already renders. Note the second option changes what the non-collapsed view means, so it is a behaviour decision rather than a pure fix.<br><br>**A second, structural finding in the same method, not separately logged because it has the same fix site:** `BeginScrollView` does **not** cull - it lays out every child and clips afterwards - so all 1000 rows get full text measurement on every Layout pass. Measured by review as roughly **13,000 allocations/frame, ~30 MB/s** at 60fps in collapsed mode, dominated by per-row `GUILayoutOption` construction from `ExpandWidth(true)`/`Width(50)` plus `count.ToString()`. Hoisting two `static readonly GUILayoutOption[]` and caching the count string is cheap; range-clipping the drawn rows to the scroll viewport is the structural fix.<br><br>Found by `performance-reviewer` 2026-08-28 while reviewing L36; verified by reading `ProcessLogItem` and `TrimExcessLogs` directly | `Assets/01_Scripts/EnigmaVirtual/Development/Console.cs:294` (the unbounded loop), `:443-447` (`ProcessLogItem` coalescing), `:458-465` (`TrimExcessLogs`), `:322-327` (the unculled scroll view) <br><br>**Fixed 2026-08-28 (v0.55.5).** The repeat loop is bounded by a `maxRepeatDraw` const of 50, and the truncation is **reported rather than silent** - an overflow row carries the true total in the same badge the collapsed view uses, because a cap you cannot see reads exactly like a message that stopped repeating. `count` is now rendered once at the point it changes (`Log.countText`, with `CountText` falling back to a live conversion) instead of per row per pass.<br><br>**Three allocation fixes landed with it**, all on the same draw path and none changing behaviour: `Enum.GetValues(typeof(LogType))` is hoisted to a `static readonly LogType[]` instead of allocating an array on every toolbar pass; the two per-row `GUILayout.ExpandWidth(true)`/`Width(50)` options are hoisted to `static readonly GUILayoutOption[]`; and the per-row `_logStyle.normal.background` write - a managed-to-native setter running twice per row per frame - is replaced by two prebuilt styles, `_logStyleEven`/`_logStyleOdd`, selected by reference.<br><br>**Verified in play mode with a real 100,000-repeat entry** injected by reflection. The assertion is the scroll content size, because `BeginScrollView` clamps `scrollPosition.y` to content height minus viewport: with one entry of `count = 100000` it clamps to **648.5** units, about 28 rows of overflow, consistent with 51 drawn rows. Unbounded, the same entry would produce roughly **2,300,000** units - six orders of magnitude larger. The editor held 38-64 fps throughout. Four assemblies build `0 Error(s)` **present**.<br><br>**The scroll view is virtualised too, as of v0.55.6.** `BeginScrollView` lays out every child and clips afterwards, so 1000 entries used to get full text measurement on every layout pass. Each display entry is now measured once into a cumulative-offset list, the visible range is found by binary search, and two `GUILayout.Space` calls stand in for the rows above and below - which keeps the inner rect's true height, so the scrollbar proportions and `IsScrolledToBottom` still work against real geometry. Heights are rebuilt only when the filtered set, font size, collapse mode or content width changes; the per-frame path is O(log n + visible).<br><br>**Two things this had to get right.** The range is resolved on the **Layout pass only** and reused by every other pass, because IMGUI requires Layout and Repaint to emit identical controls and `scrollPosition` can move between them - getting this wrong produces `Mismatched LayoutGroup` errors. And when the whole content fits the viewport it draws everything, so short lists behave exactly as before.<br><br>**Measured in play mode, 1000 entries of deliberately varied height** (some wrapping, some not): **27 entries drawn instead of 1000**, and the visible window tracks scroll correctly - y=0 gives [0,26), y=10000 gives [240,266), y=20000 gives [482,509), and the bottom reaches index 1000 so nothing is unreachable. Correlated against the screen: at [482,509) the viewport shows VIRT 483 onward. Frame rate over a 6.8s window is **52.7 fps with 1000 entries against 54.2 fps with 5** - within noise, which is the actual claim: cost no longer depends on how many entries exist. Zero `Mismatched LayoutGroup` or any other console error. Counter-test: 5 entries draws all 5.<br><br>**Not measured: the pre-fix 1000-entry frame rate.** Getting it would mean reverting the file under a live editor, which this project has been bitten by before, so the A/B above is against list length rather than against the old code.<br><br>**A trap for anyone re-running this**: the first probe reported `Log` as having no `countText` field, which reads as the edit not having landed. It had - `dotnet build` does not write `Library/ScriptAssemblies`, so `eval` was running against Unity's stale assembly. Recompile in Unity and confirm the member names are in the dll before trusting a reflection probe | **fixed 2026-08-28** (v0.55.5, virtualisation v0.55.6) |
| M122 | **The in-game console never followed new messages, so an operator watching it saw the oldest entries and nothing else.** `Console.IsScrolledToBottom` decided whether to keep tracking the tail with `Mathf.Approximately(innerH, scrollPosition.y + outerH)`. `Mathf.Approximately`'s epsilon is relative - at a content height of ~6000 px it is about **0.006 px** - and the two sides are derived differently, one of them net of `GUI.skin.box.padding.vertical`. They never agreed, so `ScrollToBottom` never fired.<br><br>**Measured before the fix**: with 200 entries seeded and the console open, `scrollPosition.y` stayed at **0.0** while `filteredLogs` grew to 206 - the view sat on the oldest entry and never moved. Starting deliberately *at* the bottom did not help either: y froze at 5030.8 while the true bottom moved to 5057.5, so the console fell behind the moment anything was logged.<br><br>**A second cause, and the reason the first one alone would not have fixed it.** The view opens at scroll 0, and following only ever resumes once you are already at the end - so even with a working test it could never catch up on its own. Opening now jumps to the newest message (`SetVisible`, used by the hotkey, the shake gesture and `openOnStart`).<br><br>**Fixed 2026-08-28 (v0.55.7).** The equality is now a tolerance against the real maximum - `scrollPosition.y >= maxScroll - logFontSize * 1.5f`, a band of one row, so float drift at the end does not detach the view while scrolling up by a line does. Content shorter than the viewport returns true, as before.<br><br>**`ScrollToBottom` deliberately keeps its `int.MaxValue` sentinel rather than computing the offset**, and this was measured the wrong way round first: replacing it with `innerScrollRect.height - outerScrollRect.height` looked tidier and landed **one row short of the end**, because the outer rect includes a border the visible viewport does not - which is precisely what the old code's `- GUI.skin.box.padding.vertical` was compensating for. Only `BeginScrollView` knows the exact clamp. `ResolveVisibleRange` clamps the sentinel before searching, so the frame before IMGUI clamps it cannot resolve to an empty range.<br><br>**Verified in play mode, four states**: following - range `[184,216)` with `lastIndex` 215 included; scrolled up to y=2000 - detaches to `[70,108)`; four messages logged while scrolled up - y stays exactly **2000.0** and the range does not move, so the view is not yanked away from someone reading history; scrolled back to the end - re-attaches and follows again. Confirmed visually: a marker logged last renders flush above the toolbar. Zero console errors. Four assemblies build `0 Error(s)` **present**.<br><br>Pre-existing - untouched by the v0.55.4-v0.55.6 console work, which changed neither method. Reported by the user 2026-08-28 | `Assets/01_Scripts/EnigmaVirtual/Development/Console.cs` - `IsScrolledToBottom`, `ScrollToBottom`, `SetVisible`, and the three `isVisible` toggle sites in `Start`/`Update` | **fixed 2026-08-28** (v0.55.7) |
| M123 | **The shipped `settings.json` had `cleanUnlistedAssets` back ON, committed by a change whose own message says "No code change".** `cb24c5f` (2026-08-19, *Test plan executed: L27 confirmed, M118 relocated*) flipped two values in `Assets/StreamingAssets/config/settings.json` - `AppData.Cache.AssetDownload.cleanUnlistedAssets` `false`->`true` and `Sockets.isActive` `false`->`true` - while stating no code changed. Every project branched between 2026-08-19 and this fix inherited it.<br><br>**Three places asserted the opposite while it was true**, which is why nobody reading the code would have caught it: `CLAUDE.md` ("`false` again as of v0.30.0"), `AssetManager.cs:306` ("which ships false") and `AppDataController.cs:436` ("Dormant only while `cleanUnlistedAssets` ships false"). All three are accurate again now, which is the argument for fixing the config rather than rewriting the comments to match a state nobody wants.<br><br>**Fixed 2026-08-28: one value, `true` -> `false`.** No code change - the C# default at `AppDataSettings.cs:514` was already `false`; only the shipped config disagreed. The gate worked; nothing else did. The lesson is about the detection delay, not the gate: a config value can regress and sit in the template indefinitely, because nothing checks the shipped config until somebody tries a **release** build, and development builds skip that rule by design.<br><br>**Not verified: that a release build now passes the L12 rule.** The editor was closed by the time the fix landed. Reading the field per subscriber makes the last write win everywhere. Also from review: the failure log named `typeof(T)`, i.e. the *value* type, which identifies nothing when three MonoBehaviours subscribe to one Observable - it names the failing method now, after a `LogException` (M80).<br><br>`EqualityComparer<T>.Default` was checked against the Unity case specifically: for a `UnityEngine.Object` it resolves to `ObjectEqualityComparer`, whose null checks are plain reference comparisons, so a destroyed object compares *unequal* to null and notifies - more notifications than `==` would give, never fewer | `Observable.cs:52`, `Observable.cs:70`, `Observable.cs:92` | **fixed 2026-08-14** (v0.49.0), dormant here |
| L5 | **All of it fixed - and my first attempt turned the traversal fix into data loss.**<br><br>`SavePath` is `DestinationPath + DestinationName`, bare concatenation of two caller/CMS values, so a name carrying `..` writes anywhere. Contained the way H22 does it: `GetFullPath` both sides, then require the result under the root - **before** the request, because the body is streamed to that path and by write time it is too late.<br><br>**The regression:** streaming with `DownloadHandlerFile` removes the double in-memory copy, but that handler **creates and truncates its target before the response arrives** - so a 404 replaced good cached media with the error body, where the old `WriteAllBytes` at least only touched disk on success. It streams to `.part` now and moves into place only once the request succeeded.<br><br>That `.part` also answers the second finding: a `using` inside a coroutine is `try`/`finally`, and Unity **abandons** an abandoned iterator without running it (M95, measured in this project) - so the request and the file handle leak on `StopCoroutine`/`SetActive(false)`/destroy. My remark had claimed the `using` "covers every exit". It does not; what makes it survivable is that an abandoned download leaves a stray `.part`, never a truncated real file.<br><br>Also: `timeout` was 0, i.e. wait forever, while `FileController` chains its list on `onFileComplete` - one unreachable host stalled every remaining file silently (M43's trap again). `redirectLimit` dropped from 32 to 4. `OrdinalIgnoreCase` in the containment check is wrong on Android and Linux, where `/data/media/` and `/data/MEDIA/` are different directories - platform-selected now. The check uses `Path.Combine` rather than `SavePath`, so a `DestinationPath` without a trailing separator is not silently refused. The request is disposed, the dead `while (!www.isDone)` is gone, the `[Header]` on a non-MonoBehaviour is gone, and `downloadFile` is `DownloadFile` with an `[Obsolete]` forwarder.<br><br>And in `DownloadVO`: **both constructors called `Directory.CreateDirectory` on the unvalidated CMS path** - so merely *describing* a download created arbitrary directory trees anywhere writable, from a constructor nobody guards. Removed; the folder is created after containment instead | `FileDownloader.cs:214`, `FileDownloader.cs:63`, `FileDownloader.cs:129` | **fixed 2026-08-14** (v0.49.1), dormant here |
| L6 | **Closed. Four of the five done; the namespace sweep split out as L17.**<br><br>`appDataMaxLenght` is `appDataMaxLength`. Renaming a settings field is normally a config-format change - the field name **is** the JSON key, and it also invalidates any PlayerPrefs snapshot (M54, H14) - but this key is **not present in the shipped `settings.json`**, so no deployed machine carries it and the C# initialiser has always been what applied. Established by grepping the shipped file rather than assuming.<br><br>The settings class `EditorSettings` is now `PanelAccessSettings`. **The field on `CoreSettings` keeps the name `EditorSettings`**, because that is the JSON key - so no settings file changes anywhere. Verified against the shipped file: the block still parses, a value inside it still binds, and the new *type* name is **not** accepted as a key. Named for what it holds - password, maxTries, lockoutSeconds, autoHideTime - after a first attempt called it `ConfigPanelSettings`, one letter from the unrelated `ConfigsPanel`, which does not read it.<br><br>**`AnaliticsSender` was renamed and then reverted, because M8 had already decided it should not be.** M8: *"class-name typo stays. Renaming a public framework type breaks every branched project for a spelling fix, and L6 tracks the naming pass as one batch."* I reversed that without reading it, and half-way - the class became `AnalyticsSender` while its public `SendAnalitics` stayed misspelled, which is worse for a branch than either endpoint. Reverted. If it is to be reversed it is one batch, class and members together, and M8 cites Tiago on keeping the class.<br><br>The two `DetectLongClick.cs` were settled by M59<br><br>**Done, and the reversal is Tiago's** (2026-08-14). M8 had recorded his decision that the class-name typo should stay; he reversed it explicitly after being shown the cost, so the naming pass ran as the one batch this entry always required. `AnaliticsSender` is `AnalyticsSender`, `SendAnalitics` is `SendAnalytics`, `DoAnaliticsTest` is `DoAnalyticsTest`, and the separate `OneCoreClient` VO `Analitics` is `AnalyticsPayload`, with `OneCore.SendAnalitics` following it. Both files moved with `git mv` **including their `.meta`**, so the script GUIDs are unchanged and any scene reference in a branched project survives the move - there is none here, verified by grepping both GUIDs across `Assets/` and `ProjectSettings/`.<br><br>**The VO is `AnalyticsPayload`, not `Analytics`, and that is deliberate.** `UnityEngine.Analytics.Analytics` is a real type - `AnalyticsSender` calls `Analytics.CustomEvent` on it - so a type named `Analytics` in a namespace someone imports beside `UnityEngine.Analytics` is a `CS0104`, surfacing in a **branched project's** file rather than in this one. Fixing a spelling is not worth buying a collision; this is the same hazard L17 tracks generally.<br><br>**Verified symmetrically, because a half-rename is the thing this entry warned about**: 11 of 11 by reflection - all four new names resolve, all three old names are gone, the `[ContextMenu]` survives, the component still `AddComponent`s, and driving `SendAnalytics` still raises the `LogError` naming the **new** class (that report is M8's actual fix, and losing it silently while renaming would have been the worst outcome here). | `DebugSettings.cs:44`, `AppDataSettings.cs:57`, `AnalyticsSender.cs:7`, `AnalyticsSender.cs:55`, `AnalyticsPayload.cs:19`, `OneCore.cs:330` | **fixed 2026-08-14** (v0.49.5) |
| L7 | Magic numbers / hardcoded values. ~~1s RFID timeout~~ (serialized v0.47.0, M15); the two `CallLater(..., 5f)` delays in `AppDataController`; ~~`new Texture2D(128,128)`~~; the hardcoded Chrome UA; `maxRetryCount` non-serialized; `"Textures/ImageDownloadError"` repeated.<br><br>**Each one named rather than changed, which is the point of the entry.** The UA is byte-identical - it still claims **Chrome 72** (2019), itself a filtering signal to a modern CDN, but changing what a deployed site sends is a behaviour change for every branched project, so it is now a `const` a reader can see and decide about. The two delays are `const` rather than serialized: the cooldown one gates dead code (M97, its only reader is a commented-out `Update`), so an inspector knob would advertise a setting that changes nothing.<br><br>**The repeat count was wrong, and measuring it changed the fix.** The entry said 6x since v0.4.0. Measured at HEAD: **4**, across two files - and only **2** are `Resources.Load` calls, the other 2 being the path spelled inside error-message strings. Those two rot most quietly, because a message naming a path that no longer exists sends the next reader to a file that is fine. Had the 6 been trusted, the search would have been for two call sites that do not exist.<br><br>**`new Texture2D(128,128)` was already gone** - the only match left is the comment recording its removal, so that clause closes by inspection, not by an edit.<br><br>**`maxRetryCount` is serialized, and review found the hazard that came with it.** `CoreSetup.unity` carries an authored `AssetManager`, so the first question was whether a newly-exposed int lands as a serialized `0` and silently disables retries. It does not: the component has **zero** serialized field entries in the scene YAML, and Unity applies YAML over a normally-constructed object, so an absent key never overwrites the initialiser - it loads as 3, verified live. The consequence is the *next* one: the first time anyone opens and saves that scene, `3` bakes in and the initialiser goes decorative for the authored instance, so changing the framework default later reaches a branched project only if it never saved. `[Range(0, 10)]` bounds it at 11 attempts x 60s = **11 minutes per dead asset**.<br><br>**Two claims corrected before they shipped, both mine.** The delay's remark said "the C3 watchdog budget is 120s" - arithmetically right (`Max(90, timeout * 2)` at the shipped 60s) but describing a *total*, which does not exist: `BootWatchdog_IE` measures the gap between `_bootStage` changes, so 5s spends **stall** budget inside one stage. And the `[Range]` was first justified as stopping a boot-stalling typo. **It does not, and M70 already said so**: `onAssetDownloadEvent` carries the *raw* `validatedAssets`, so every retry moves the stage string and resets the deadline. A `maxRetryCount` of 50 makes a dead asset take ~51 minutes; it never stalls. The cap is against a boot nobody waits out, not a hang.<br><br>Sibling literal `"Textures/videoDownloadError"` left alone and split out as **L18** - single-use, so `AppResources`' own admission rule keeps it at its call site, but it derefs `.texture` unguarded | `AssetManager.cs:44`, `AssetManager.cs:57`, `AppDataController.cs:138`, `AppDataController.cs:153`, `AppResources.cs:28` | **fixed 2026-08-14** (v0.49.3) |
| L8 | **The headline clause turned out not to be a defect, and acting on it would have made things worse.** The entry read "`.webp` is missing from the accepted image extensions, though the sample assets include one", carried since v0.4.0. Both halves are wrong.<br><br>**Unity cannot decode webp**, so the exclusion is the decoder's capability rather than a preference. Measured in this editor against a Pillow-generated webp, with a PNG of the identical image as the control: `DownloadHandlerTexture.GetContent` returns **null** with `result = DataProcessingError`, and `Texture2D.LoadImage` returns **false**, while the PNG decodes. Supporting webp means shipping a decoder, not editing an extension list.<br><br>**And no `.webp` has ever existed in this repo** - `git log --all --diff-filter=A -- '*.webp'` is empty across every branch, so "the sample assets include one" was never true. The only `webp` string in the tree is a `*.webp binary` line in the stock `.gitattributes`, which is probably where the belief came from.<br><br>**Review corrected the reason while confirming the decision, which is the part worth keeping.** The first version of this note said admitting `.webp` would produce a *blank texture*. That describes `Texture2D.LoadImage` - which has **zero call sites in this codebase**. Every image path goes through `DownloadHandlerTexture`, whose null is reported at `LogError` by all three consumers. So the real cost of admitting it would be a per-image download and a per-image error in place of one aggregate rejection: worse, but not silent. A wrong reason recorded in a comment is how a settled decision gets re-opened by the next person who measures, so it is now stated correctly in both copies of the set.<br><br>**The clause that WAS real is the one the entry claimed was already fixed.** The parenthetical said the per-call `HashSet` allocation was fixed in v0.2.0 - that landed in `TextureCacheManager` only. `MediaFile`'s three predicates each still built a fresh `HashSet` on **every call**; all three are `static readonly` now.<br><br>**Sized honestly, it is tidiness rather than a performance win.** Measured at 200k iterations: 3635 ns / ~224 B per call before, 1622 ns / ~32 B after - **~2.0 us and 192 B saved**. Nothing on these paths is per-frame; the busiest is one call per gallery item, on a frame already instantiating that many prefabs, so the saving is **under 1%** of the per-item cost. Recorded because the register has carried an unmeasured performance claim about this file once already. (A `switch`/`EndsWith` form would be ~10x faster per call than a 3-element `OrdinalIgnoreCase` `HashSet`, whose single probe costs 447 ns against 53 ns for an `Ordinal` one - deliberately not done: unobservable here, and the shared set is the readable form.) | `MediaFile.cs:35`, `TextureCacheManager.cs:432` | **fixed 2026-08-14** (v0.49.4) |
| L9 | **Fixed by hoisting the `Regex`, and explicitly NOT by the cache this entry asked for.** `assetsPath` called the static `Regex.Replace` on every access and `GetAssetPath` reads it once per asset. Measured in the editor before changing anything: **4.9us per call**, so about **2.2ms once** across a 500-asset walk (0.45ms at 100 assets, 9ms at 2000).<br><br>A cached path string - the entry's prescription - would be a **correctness** bug for that 2.2ms. Every input to the getter is rewritten at runtime when the settings panel saves: `Cache.AssetDownload.isActive`, `AppData.downloadFolder`, and both the local and remote settings blocks. There is no existing change signal to invalidate against, so the cache would serve a stale asset root after any settings save - the class of defect this register mostly exists to catch.<br><br>Hoisting the instance is the whole fix: **4.9us to 3.3us**, a third off, portable everywhere. `RegexOptions.Compiled` reaches 2.7us but needs Reflection.Emit, which IL2CPP does not have - and Android ships IL2CPP - so that last 0.7us would exist only under Mono. Not taken.<br><br>The lookbehind is load-bearing and was verified rather than assumed: a plain `path.Replace("//", "/")` turns `https://cms.example.com//assets` into `https:/cms.example.com/assets`. Measured, both before and after, with identical output from all three variants | `AppDataSettings.cs:41`, `AppDataSettings.cs:324` | **fixed 2026-08-14** (v0.47.3) |
| L10 | Process management catches only `Win32Exception`/`FileNotFoundException` — misses `InvalidOperationException` from the `HasExited`→`Kill()` race and `NotSupportedException`; `MainModule.FileName` throws under restricted tokens; killing explorer is unlogged<br><br>**Fixed 2026-08-17 (v0.49.12), and the worst part of it was not the filter.** The try/catch sat **outside both loops**, so the first process that refused to die abandoned every remaining name in the operator's `killProcesses` list - one protected process silently cancelled the rest of the request. It is now per-process, so a failure reports and the loop continues, and the message names which process and says it is continuing.<br><br>**And that fix left the same defect one level up**, which `code-reviewer` caught: the outer catch was re-scoped to `GetProcessesByName` but still sat *outside the name loop*, so an enumeration failure on the first name still abandoned every remaining one - verbatim the abandonment the change was made to remove. There are now two nested trys, each scoped to one thing (`MainController.cs:409`): one per name for the enumeration, one per process for the kill.<br><br>**The filter now admits `InvalidOperationException` and `NotSupportedException` at all eight process catches** (`MainController.cs:287` and seven more). The race is measured rather than asserted: a process started with `cmd /c exit`, waited for, then `Kill()`ed throws exactly `InvalidOperationException` - which is neither `Win32Exception` nor `FileNotFoundException`, so it escaped the method and took the caller's boot step with it. A counter-test confirms the filter is still a whitelist: `ArgumentNullException` would propagate.<br><br>**Killing the shell is now announced before it happens** (`MainController.cs:484`), at `LogError` and unconditionally - and the sentence reads `restoreExplorerOnQuit` rather than assuming it. The first version promised "OnDestroy restarts it, so a clean exit restores the shell" unconditionally, which is false whenever that setting is off: the one message an operator relies on when the desktop disappears would have promised a restore that never happens - `doLogs` ships `false`, which is precisely when this line is needed. The `taskkill` call was previously unguarded *and* unlogged, so a blocked or missing `taskkill.exe` threw straight out of the boot sequence and "the desktop is gone" reached whoever read the log with nothing to connect it to. Now wrapped, and the returned `Process` is disposed (`MainController.cs:496`) - it never was, leaking a handle on every boot that kills the shell. `process?.Dispose()` also moved into a `finally` (`MainController.cs:458`), because a process that had already exited skipped the old dispose.<br><br>**`MainModule.FileName` deliberately unchanged.** The entry lists it, but `FullRestart` already catches bare `Exception` and logs the full exception, so a restricted token is reported rather than silent - which the entry's own closing note says. Nothing to do.<br><br>**What is not verified**: the kill loop is never invoked by the probe, because invoking it kills whatever the operator listed. Its restructure is checked at source level and the exception type it now catches is measured; the loop running end to end is a play-mode step against a throwaway process, written into the test plan | `MainController.cs:409`, `MainController.cs:422`, `MainController.cs:482`, `MainController.cs:458` | **fixed 2026-08-17** (v0.49.12) |
| L11 | No test assembly. Blockers: static singletons, MonoBehaviour-bound logic, direct `File.*`/`UnityWebRequest` in coroutines, `internal` with no `InternalsVisibleTo` | project-wide | **open** |
| L12 | `Cache.AssetDownload.cleanUnlistedAssets` was guarded by prose only - a derived project could ship `true` with nothing stopping it, and that flag makes the app recursively delete every unreferenced file under the asset root on an unattended machine. `BuildValidator` now fails a release build whose shipped `settings.json` enables it. Enabling it on a specific installation's *deployed* config is still entirely fine - the gate is about what gets baked into a build.<br><br>**Revised 2026-08-28 (v0.56.2): it warns rather than fails, because the premise above was too narrow.** A release build is often **pre-configured for one site**, and then a baked-in `cleanUnlistedAssets` is exactly what was intended - so "not something to bake into a build" describes one deployment model rather than the only one. The design flaw is visible next to H14 in the same method: a client needing a real panel password has a legitimate spelling (`passwordHash`) and the build passes, while a client wanting asset cleanup had **no way through at all** short of disabling the validator or editing the config after the build - which is worse, because it is unreviewable. Now the same shape as the E2 schema check: legitimate exceptions exist, so it reports instead of refusing, and the message says both what the flag will do on first run and that it is a mistake in the *template*.<br><br>**Verified by two release builds.** Flag `false`: 1 failure, H14 only, no L12 line. Flag `true`: still 1 failure, still H14 only, and `cleanUnlistedAssets` appears among the warnings (3 -> 4). So a pre-configured site build proceeds once its password is set.<br><br>**What this gives up, stated plainly**: this rule is the only thing that has ever caught the flag being wrong in the template - twice, H21 and M123 - and a warning will not stop a third. It never caught either one promptly anyway (both found months late, by accident, because nothing here builds release routinely), so the protection was already weaker than it looked; the honest place for a template check is something that runs on every commit | `BuildValidator.cs` `CheckShippedSettings`, `settings.json` | **fixed 2026-08-10** (v0.11.0), **revised to a warning 2026-08-28** (v0.56.2) |

## Enhancements

Requested work that is not a defect. Same ID discipline, `E` prefix, so the worklist can carry them without them counting as bugs.

| ID | Request | Location | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| E1 | **An operator key that skips asset validation at boot.** `Ctrl+Alt+K` by default, configurable through `System.Windows.skipAssetValidationKeyCode`. Sets a sticky one-way `Core.SkipAssetValidation`, honoured in three places: before the phase starts (so a press during appData load counts), per asset inside the download loop (so it takes effect at the next iteration rather than the next chunk), and in `ValidateExistingMedia`, where cleanup is skipped outright - cleanup would probably be safe, but "probably safe" plus "deletes files" plus "a human just hit the panic key" is not worth reasoning about at a site.<br><br>**The register's original design note was wrong** and is corrected here: it said the key must not depend on `settings.json` parsing, per H17. But validation is only reached *after* settings parse, so that is not the risk. The real one is a config **deployed before this feature existed**, where the absent key deserializes to 0 = `KeyCode.None` and `GetKeyDown(None)` never fires - so the hatch would be missing on exactly the older installs most likely to need it. Hence the hardcoded fallback, which is the mechanism rather than a safety net: H5's patcher copies keys the *shipped* file has, so until that file carries the key there is nothing to copy.<br><br>Review caught that the first version was silent and over-claiming in the ways the feature exists to prevent: a skipped boot looked identical to a clean one (no notification, boot stage still read "assets validated"); the abandoned tail was counted as "will be MISSING", the same over-claim removed from M34 120 lines below, so a fully cached kiosk skipped at asset 3 of 225 would have reported 222 missing; the `yield break` dropped the stale and unparseable-header tallies; the cleanup-skipped line was `LogWarning`, stripped without `DO_LOGS`, for a step that deletes files; and pressing the key after boot finished still claimed a degradation that had not happened. All fixed. Every press now raises a notification, including repeats, because the in-flight request must finish first and the operator otherwise sees nothing for up to a minute. **Caveat:** the key is in the working-tree `settings.json` but the key reached the shipped template with v0.15.0 (local debug values, H14 material), so the *shipped* template does not carry it yet - the fallback covers it until someone commits that line | `Core.cs`, `MainController.cs`, `AssetManager.cs`, `SystemSettings.cs` | **done 2026-08-11** (v0.14.0) |
| E2 | `BuildValidator` now cross-checks the shipped settings.json against `CoreSettings`: every key is walked beside the C# type that would receive it, and any key no field can receive is reported. This class of bug is invisible by construction - `JsonUtility` matches by field name and says nothing about a key it cannot place - which is why M54 survived the life of the project and was found only by accident.<br><br>**Warn-only, not a gate.** Legitimately unreachable keys exist (M55's `dynamic` fields), so a hard failure would have shipped with a suppression list, and that list is what turns a useful rule into one somebody deletes. It runs on every build rather than release-only, because a dead key is dead in a development build too, and warnings are emitted before the failure throw so they survive a failing build.<br><br>Reflection over public instance fields, which is exactly what `JsonUtility` reads - properties are invisible to it (M3), as is anything `[NonSerialized]`. Inherited fields are included, which matters: `OneCoreClient.Settings : SocketsSettings` gets eleven of its keys from the base, and a check using `DeclaredOnly` would have reported all eleven as orphans.<br><br>**Verified by mutation test rather than by inspection.** Six cases, all behaving as specified: the real config clean; M54's exact shape caught; an operator's case-typo (`api_Host`→`api_host`) caught; a typo three levels deep caught, proving the walk descends; an undeclared top-level block caught; and a typo *inside* `serialPorts[0]` **not** caught, which is the documented limit rather than a surprise.<br><br>**Known limits, named in the rule's own message** so a clean result is not read as broader than it is: array elements are not descended into (the same choice `ConfigManager.PatchDeployed` makes), and only settings.json against `CoreSettings` is checked - not `appData.json`, which is Newtonsoft with different matching rules.<br><br>It also reports, at `Log` rather than warning level, scalar settings that exist in `CoreSettings` but not in the shipped file - knobs an operator cannot turn because they were never given them, which is what E1 ran into. Not a defect list: an absent key keeps the C# initializer by design. Three today: `AppData.appDataMaxLength` (also a spelling L6 covers), `OSC.clientName`, `SerialPorts.showNotifications` | `BuildValidator.cs:211-339`, wired in at `:53-68` | **done 2026-08-11** (v0.17.0) |

| 2 | L28 | Low | S | Low | The AssetGenerator emits **inverted z-order**: the Photoshop exporter walks layers top-first, `AssetList` parents them in that order, and a later sibling draws on top. The reversal that corrected it lived only in the copy L27 deleted. Not fixed there because restoring it changes generated output - a decision about branched projects' authored scenes - and `ReverseData()` NREs on a null `layers`. **Do not sweep `ReverseData()`**: it is the last trace of the intent |
| 3 | L31 | Low | S | Low | Two prefabs packed only because they live under `Resources/`, both byte-identical to unpacked twins in `04_Prefabs/Ui/`. Honest total for the whole pattern is **227 KB, 0.95%** - the 964 KB YAML packs to 167 KB. Fix is safe here but moving an asset out of `Resources/` breaks branches invisibly via `Factory.cs`'s composed-path load. Not worth it without a branch check |
| 4 | L35 | Low | M | Low | `Langs.cs` declares 32 languages; `AR ZH JA KO MY` have **no glyph coverage in any shipped font**, so they render blank (space substitution, not boxes). No fix without shipping a CJK/Arabic face - probably document the enum as aspirational and hide unrenderable languages in the picker |
| 5 | L37 | Low | S | Low | `Console.DrawLogList` sets four `fixedWidth` values on the **shared** `GUI.skin` every frame and never restores them, so the console's touch-widened scrollbar leaks into any other IMGUI in the process. Fix is the shape the same file already uses everywhere else - a `GUIStyle` built in `EnsureStyles` and passed to the scroll view. Tier 5: no second IMGUI surface is authored in this repo, so it is dormant here and live only for a branch that draws its own |

### Sequencing notes

Written with **issue ids, not row numbers**. Rows are renumbered on every fix, so prose citing them
rots silently: by v0.17.0 this section read `1 inside 1, 1 inside 1` - what a dozen
renumbering passes did to references the remap could no longer resolve. Ids never move.

**Tier criteria**, applied in order - the first that fits wins:

| Tier | Takes |
|---|---|
| 1 | Live in the shipped config, **or** live in any branched project, **and** S effort |
| 2 | M effort, or changes a public signature - a session's work |
| 3 | A platform other than Windows, or config/deployment resolution |
| 4 | L effort, or blocked on a decision nobody has taken |
| 5 | Dormant, no caller today, or measured as noise at current scale |

Within a tier, order by **damage if ignored**, not by severity label. Read the full entry before
ranking: on 2026-08-14 two rows moved once their entries were read, because the worklist summary
omitted that M96 is dormant here and live only in branches, and that M48's skipped event has no
subscribers, so its live half is the empty log rather than the missed event.

**A tier that empties is a tier that has stopped being maintained**, and it fails silently. Rows
drain upward as the ones above them close; by 2026-08-14 all 23 open rows had ended up under Tier 5
("not urgent") while Tiers 1-4 were empty headings. The list still rendered, still numbered
contiguously, and still passed every consistency check. Re-tier when a tier empties.

- **Tier 3's rows are each one sitting**, and are separate rows so each gets its own commit.
- **Fold-in, done in v0.20.0:** **M17** was fixed inside **C6**, as planned - one rewrite of
  `SocketConnection`, one review pass over it. Kept here as the worked example: the two entries described
  one file's threading and one file's buffering, and neither could be fixed without the other, because
  framing is what decides whether a read loop can exist at all.
- **M20 is done (v0.24.0)**, cycle and split both. It had been blocked on C4 (cleared v0.3.0)
  and then on its own framework-to-`App` cycle, broken across v0.21.1-v0.23.0.
- **L11 is unblocked as of v0.24.0** - the test assembly it needs can now reference
  `EnigmaVirtual` and `App`. Its other four blockers are untouched, so unblocked is not cheap:
  static singletons, MonoBehaviour-bound logic, `File.*`/`UnityWebRequest` inside coroutines,
  and `internal` with no `InternalsVisibleTo` - which the split has now made a real barrier
  rather than a notional one.
- **M40 is where the PlayerPrefs shadowing lives.** M53 was a duplicate and was merged into it on
  2026-08-11; do not log it a third time.
- **H18 owns the four dead Phidgets fields** in `CoreSetup.unity`. M52 made the panel stop reporting
  them, which removes the noise but not the rows.
- M26 is done (v0.8.7): `ShowNotification` logs, so a failure reported only as a toast is no longer
  invisible.

- **Re-tiered 2026-08-14.** Every open row had drained into Tier 5 as the rows above them were fixed, so 23 items - including `M96` and `M45` - sat under a heading reading *not urgent*, and Tiers 1-4 were empty headings. Ids and row text are unchanged; only placement and numbering moved. **A tier that empties is a tier that stops being maintained**, and the failure is silent: the list still renders, still numbers correctly, and quietly misfiles everything left.
